2.3.2     Holonomy along small piecewise geodesic loops
In order to estimate the holonomy along a small lasso, we need, according to the proposition
1.8.5, to know the area enclosed by its buckle. This area can be controlled by the length of the
buckle using an isoperimetric inequality. We recall a classical fact about open covering of metric
compact sets. A proof can be found in [Ma].
Lemma 2.3.6 Let M be a metric compact set. Let (Oi )i∈I be an open covering of M . There
exists a positive real number R called Lebesgue number of this covering, such that for any ball
B of radius smaller than R in M , there exists an i ∈ I such that B ⊂ Oi .
2.3. PRELIMINARY RESULTS                                                                       31

Proposition 2.3.7 There exist R > 0 and K > 0 such that any simple loop l contained in a
ball B of radius smaller than R is the boundary of an open set U ⊂ B such that
                                          σ(U ) ≤ Kℓ(l)2 .
    Proof. Let R0 be such that any closed geodesic ball of M of radius smaller than R0 is
diffeomorphic to a disk. Let B1 , . . . , Bn be a covering of M by open balls of radius R0 . Let
us denote by g the metric on M and g0 the euclidean metric on R2 . For each i, there exists
a diffeomorphism φi : Bi −→ D(0, 1) ⊂ R2 . Since the metrics g and φ∗ g0 can be compared on
Bi , the usual isoperimetric inequality on D(0, 1) gives rise to an inequality on Bi , with some
constant Ki . Let K be the supremum of K1 , . . . , Kn . Let R be a Lebesgue number of the
covering B1 , . . . , Bn . Then the statement holds with this choice of K and R.

   Now we can estimate the holonomy along a small lasso:
Proposition 2.3.8 There exist L0 > 0 and K > 0 such that if λ is a piecewise geodesic lasso
whose buckle β has a length smaller than L0 , then
                                 Eρ(Hλ ) = Ed(Hλ , 1) ≤ Kℓ(β).

    Proof. The lasso λ can be written σβσ −1 , so thanks to invariance by conjugation of the
distance on G, we have Eρ(Hλ ) = Eρ(Hβ ). Let L0 be shorter than the shortest length of a
loop non homotopic to a point and also shorter than the radius R given py the proposition
2.3.7. Then the hypothesis ℓ(β) ≤ L0 implies that β is the boundary of a small disk D. Using
proposition 1.8.5, we get                   q
                                Eρ(Hβ ) ≤ C σ(D) ≤ Kℓ(β).
   This result suggests that it will be possible to prove regularity results for the random holon-
omy using the following distance between G-valued random variables:

Definition 2.3.9 Let X and Y be two G-valued random variables defined on the same probability
space. The distance dP (X, Y ) is defined by:
                                     dP (X, Y ) = Ed(X, Y ),
where d is the biinvariant Riemannian distance on G.
   The first example of such regularity results is the following one:

Proposition 2.3.10 Let ζ be a piecewise geodesic loop of length smaller than L0 . Then
                                       dP (Hζ , 1) ≤ Kℓ(ζ).
   Proof. Since ζ is piecewise geodesic, it has finite self-intersection. So it is equivalent to a
product of piecewise geodesic lassos: ζ ≃ λ1 . . . λp . This gives:
                dP (Hζ , 1) = dP (Hλ1 . . . Hλp , 1)
                            ≤ dP (Hλ1 . . . Hλp , Hλ2 . . . Hλp ) + . . . + dP (Hλp , 1)
                            ≤ dP (Hλ1 , 1) + . . . + dP (Hλp , 1)
                            ≤ K(ℓ(β1 ) + . . . ℓ(βp ))
                            ≤ Kℓ(ζ).
32                                       CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE

2.3.3     Double layer potential of small piecewise geodesic loops
Using the same techniques as in the preceding pararaph, we will estimate the double layer
potential of a small loop. This is the first step in the proof of the proposition 2.6.8, that will
play an important role in the study of the Abelian theory.
   Recall that the definition of the potential (see 1.9.6) depends on a Riemannian metric on M
whose Riemannian volume is equal to σ. For the moment, we only know that the potential of
any element of P M is in L2 (M, σ) (see theorem 1.9.11).

Proposition 2.3.11 Let l be a lasso with buckle b. Suppose that ℓ(b) ≤ L0 , where L0 is the
length given by 2.3.8. Then
                                    k ul kL2 ≤ Kℓ(b).

    Proof. The length L0 is such that b is necessarily the boundary of a disk D whose area
satisfies σ(D) ≤ Kℓ(b)2 . Thus, by proposition 1.9.7,
                                                         1
                                            σ(D)σ(D c )    2          1
                           k ub kL2 =                          ≤ σ(D) 2 ≤ Kℓ(b).
                                              σ(M )

Since ul = ub a.e., we have the result.

     As in the preceding paragraph, this result can be extended to loops with finite self-intersection.

Proposition 2.3.12 Let l be a loop with finite self-intersection and of length smaller than L0 .
Then
                                     k ul kL2 ≤ Kℓ(l).

    Proof. Let us write that c is equivalent to a product of lassos: c ≃ l1 . . . lp . Two paths that
are equivalent have the same double layer potential almost everywhere, so that

                              k uc kL2      ≤ k ul1 + . . . + ulp kL2
                                            ≤ k ul1 kL2 + . . . + k ulp kL2
                                            ≤ K(ℓ(b1 ) + . . . + ℓ(bp ))
                                            ≤ Kℓ(c).

    The fact that the propositions 2.3.10 and 2.3.12 are very similar will allow us later, in
proposition 2.5.3 for example, to transpose directly some regularity results about the random
variables H to the double layer potential.

2.3.4     Topology on the space of paths
According to the proposition 1.8.5, it seems to be necessary to control the surface left between
two loops in order to control the distance between their holonomies. Given a Riemannian metric
on M , the uniform distance defined as follows:

                                 d∞ (c1 , c2 ) = inf sup d(c1 (t), c2 (t))
                                                     t∈[0,1]
2.4. APPROXIMATION OF EMBEDDED PATHS                                                                   33

allows to control this surface, where the infimum is taken over all reparametrizations of c1 and
c2 .
     In the paper [Be], C. Becker says that the double layer potential of a loop depends con-
tinuously of this loop in norm L2 when the set of loops on M is endowed with the topology
induced by the uniform norm. His proposition 3.2 depends on the validity of this assertion,
which is probably true if one restricts to simple loops, but not if one allows loops to have a
self-intersection, even a finite one. Let us describe a counterexample. Becker stated his result
on R2 , but this does not change the situation very much. Let M be the sphere S 2 embedded as
usual in R3 , endowed with the standard metric. Let us consider the pencil of planes (Pt )t∈[0,4π)
containing the horizontal line z = 0, y = −1, indexed in the following way: denoting by Ct
the intersection of S 2 with the lower half-space bounded by Pt , we have σ(Ct ) = t. For any
t ∈ [0, 4π), denote by ct a loop based at (0, −1, 0) whose image is the intersection of Pt with S 2 ,
oriented negatively with respect to the z axis. Let 0 < t1 < . . . < tn < 4π be n distinct times
                                                     ti
and set c = ct1 . . . ctn . For each i, uci = 1Cti − 4π . Thus,

                                                       (t1 + . . . + tn )2
         k uc k2L2   = k 1Ct1 + . . . + 1Ctn k2L2 +                2
                                                          16π                                   
                                                           t1 + . . . + tn
                                                      −2                   , 1Ct1 + . . . + 1Ctn
                                                                  4π                              L2
                                  n2 t21   n2 t2n
                     ≥ n 2 t1 +          −        .
                                  16π 2     2π
    Suppose that t1 = n1 and tn = n2 . Then k uc k2L2 is of the order of n, so it grows to infinity
when n tends to infinity. But at the same time, the loop c tends to the constant loop equal to
(0, −1, 0) in the topology induced by the distance d∞ . The potential of this constant loop being
equal to zero, this contradicts the continuity.
    Therefore, it is necessary to endow the space of paths with a topology finer than that induced
by d∞ if we expect some kind of continuity of the double layer potential and of the random
holonomy. It has emerged in the last paragraphs that the length plays a role in the continuity
results.

Definition 2.3.13 On the set of paths P M , we define the distance d1 by
                              d1 (c1 , c2 ) = d∞ (c1 , c2 ) + |ℓ(c1 ) − ℓ(c2 )|.

Proposition 2.3.14 The topology induced on P M by the distance d1 does not depend on the
Riemannian metric chosen on M .

    Proof. By compactness of M , two different metrics induce two equivalent Riemannian
distances on M and thus two equivalent distances d1 on P M .



2.4     Approximation of embedded paths
We want to extend the definition of the random holonomy to all paths in P M , by approximation.
Since any path of P M is, by definition, a concatenation of embedded submanifolds of M , it is
natural to begin with those paths who are embedded submanifolds themselves.
34                                     CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE

2.4.1    Tubular neighbourhoods and Fermi coordinates
These paths have the following nice property: they possess a tubular neighbourhood that can
be described using Fermi coordinates. Let us fix a path c which is an embedded submanifold.
The proof of the following result can be found in [Gy]. Let us fix a parametrization of c and a
vector field N along c, unitary and normal to c.

Proposition 2.4.1 There exists a positive real number r such that the mapping

                             ψ : [0, 1] × [−r, r] −→ M
                                       (t, s)         7−→ expc(t) (sNc(t) )

is a diffeomorphism onto its image, which is called tubular neighbourhood of c or tube around c.
The coordinates (t, s) are called Fermi coordinates. They satisfy:
    1. For any fixed t0 , the curve s 7→ ψ(t0 , s) is a piece of geodesic normal to c.
    2. For any couple (t, s) ∈ [0, 1] × [−r, r], d(ψ(t, s), c([0, 1])) = s.

   We shall always assume that the radius of the tubular neighbourhoods that we consider are
smaller than the convexity radius RM of M , defined in 2.2.3.

2.4.2    Piecewise geodesic approximation
The path c is fixed until the end of the next section, together with a tubular neighbourhood of
radius r.

Proposition 2.4.2 Let x, y, z be three real numbers such that 0 ≤ x < y < z ≤ r. There exists
a piecewise geodesic path σ such that
    1. σ(0) = ψ(0, y) and σ(1) = ψ(1, y),
    2. σ((0, 1)) ⊂ ψ((0, 1) × (x, z)),
    3. σ is injective.

   We construct σ as an approximation of the path cy : t 7→ ψ(t, y), in the same way as one
would approximate a curve in R2 by piecewise linear paths.
                                                                  
                                                   k            k+1
Lemma 2.4.3 Set δn (cy ) =       sup    d cy         , cy                  . Then
                              0≤k≤n−1              n             n

                                                δn (cy ) −→ 0.
                                                      n→∞

     Proof: The norm of the velocity of c is bounded.

    For n large enough and for each k = 1 . . . n, the points cy ( k−1           k
                                                                    n ) and cy ( n ) are close enough
to be joined by a unique minimizing geodesic ζn,k that stays at a distance smaller than δn
of cy ( nk ). We will always assume that n is large enough for this property to be true and set
ζn = ζn,1 . . . ζn,n .

Lemma 2.4.4 For n large enough, ζn is the graph of a continuous function in Fermi coordinates.
More precisely, there exists a continuous function ϕ : [0, 1] −→ (−r, r) such that for each t ∈
[0, 1], ζn (t) = ψ(t, ϕ(t)).
2.5. RANDOM HOLONOMY ALONG EMBEDDED PATHS                                                      35

    Proof. It is sufficient to prove that each ζn,k is the graph of a continuous function defined
                                                                                              r−y
on [ k−1  k
      n , n ] and that these functions can be put together to form ϕ. Let n be such that δn < 2 .
We show that ζn stays inside the tubular neighbourhood of c.
    The first point is that ζn,k cannot meet the horizontal boundary ψ([0, 1] × {−r, r}), because
this boundary is at distance r of c and ζn,k stays at distance smaller than δn + y < r.
    The vertical part of the boundary ψ({0, 1} × [−r, r]) is made of two pieces of minimizing
geodesics, so that ζn,k , which is also minimizing, cannot meet twice one of these pieces without
belonging to the same infinite geodesic. This is impossible because the geodesics supporting the
vertical boundary meet cy only once.
    The only way ζn,k could exit the tube around c would be to exit through one piece of the
vertical boundary and get back through the other. Suppose that n is large enough for δn being
smaller than 12 d(ψ({0} × [−r, r]), ψ({1} × [−r, r])). Then the situation described above cannot
happen, since any two points of the image of ζn,k are at distance smaller than δn . So, ζn,k stays
inside the tube.
    Each vertical slice t = t0 of the tube is a minimizing piece of a geodesic that meets c only
once inside the tube, so that it meets ζn,k at most one time. Thus, ζn,k is the graph of a smooth
function defined on the segment [ k−1      k
                                       n , n ], equal to y at both end points of this segment. All
these functions can be put together to make ϕ, which is continuous.

    Proofof proposition 2.4.2. Choose n be large enough for ζn to be the graph of a function
in Fermi coordinates and such that δn < inf(z − y, y − x). As a graph, ζn is necessary injec-
tive, which is statement 3. The inequality |ϕ(t) − y| = d(ζn (t), cy ) ≤ δn shows that ζn stays in
ψ([0, 1] × (x, z)). Together with the fact that ζn meets at most once each vertical boundary, this
gives statement 2. Statement 1 is a direct consequence of the definition of ζn . So σ = ζn has all
the properties required.

1.8.5. We prove that the random holonomy along a piecewise geodesic approximation of c con-
verges in probability to a random variable and that this limit does not depend on the particular
choice of the approximation.

2.6      Random holonomy along arbitrary paths
2.6.1    Construction of the random holonomy

embedded paths, but this decomposition is far to be unique. Nevertheless, we prove that the
random variable Hcp . . . Hc1 depends only on c.

Lemma 2.6.1 Let c be a path. There exists a sequence of piecewise geodesic paths with the
same end points as c that converges to c.

sequence.

2.6.2    Continuity of the random holonomy
At this point, we constructed a random holonomy along each path c of P M . This random
holonomy is a G-valued random variable on the probability space (Ω, A, P (x1 , . . . , xq )). Let us
state some of its basic properties.

Proposition 2.6.4 Let c, c1 , c2 be elements of P M .
   1. Hc−1 = Hc−1 a.s.
   2. The random variable Hc depends only on the equivalence class of c for the relation ≃.
   3. If c1 and c2 satisfy c1 (1) = c2 (0) then Hc1c2 = Hc2 Hc1 a.s.
40                                      CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE

   Proof: Property (3) is obvious by putting together two decompositions of c1 and c2 and the
property (2) is a direct consequence of (1). To prove (1), just note that this is true for piecewise
                                                      d                   d
                                                1
geodesic paths by construction, and that if αn −→ c, then α−1 1  −1
                                                           n −→ c .
                                                    n→∞                   n→∞

    We still have to prove that the law of this random holonomy does not depend on choice of
the Riemannian metric used in the construction. For this, we need a regularity property which
is the object of the next proposition.

Proposition 2.6.5 Let c be a path of P M . For any ε > 0, there exists δ > 0 such that if c′ is
another path of P M with the same end points as c and if d1 (c, c′ ) < δ, then dP (Hc , Hc′ ) < ε.

     Proof. Let δ0 > 0 be given by the proposition 2.5.3 such that for any piecewise geodesic
path α with the same end points as c, d1 (c, α) < δ0 , implies dP (Hc , Hα ) < ε. Let δ = δ20 .
Suppose that c′ is a path of P M with the same end points as c such that d1 (c, c′ ) < δ. Let α be
a piecewise geodesic path such that, simultaneously, d1 (α, c′ ) < δ and dP (Hc′ , Hα ) < 2ε . Then
d1 (c, α) < δ0 , so that

                           dP (Hc , Hc′ ) ≤ dP (Hc , Hα ) + dP (Hα , Hc′ ) < ε.

   Let us state a result that summarizes the results of the procedure of piecewise geodesic
approximation. It is in fact the center of the continuum limit procedure. We put together the
propositions 2.6.2, 2.6.3 and 2.6.5.

Proposition 2.6.6 Let c be a path of P M . For any sequence (αn )n≥0 of piecewise geodesic
paths converging to c with fixed end points, the sequence (Hαn )n≥0 converges to a random variable
that depends only on c and that we denote by Hc . Moreover, for any ε > 0, there exists δ > 0
such that if c′ is another path of P M with the same end points as c and if d1 (c, c′ ) < δ then
dP (Hc , Hc′ ) < ε.

2.6.3    Continuity of the double layer potential (2)
One more time, we transpose directly the preceding arguments to the double layer potential and
get the following result:

Proposition 2.6.7 Let c be a path of P M . For any ε > 0, there exists δ > 0 such that if c′ is
another path of P M with the same end points as c and if d1 (c, c′ ) < δ, then k uc − uc′ kL2 < ε.


Corollary 2.6.8 Let l be a loop of LM . For any ε > 0, there exists δ > 0 such that if l′ is
another loop of LM and if d1 (l, l′ ) < δ, then k ul − ul′ kL2 < ε.

    Proof. Let δ0 be given by the preceding proposition and set δ = δ30 . Let l′ ∈ LM be such
that d1 (l, l′ ) < δ. We have in particular d(l(0), l′ (0)) < δ. Let σ be a minimizing geodesic from
l(0) to l′ (0). Then el′ = σl′ σ −1 satisfies uel′ = ul′ a.e. and d1 (el′ , l) < δ0 . Moreover, el′ has the
same end points as l. Thus,

                                   k ul − ul′ kL2 =k ul − uel′ kL2 < ε.
2.7. LAW OF THE RANDOM HOLONOMY                                                                     41

2.7     Law of the random holonomy
For the moment, we are only able to write down the law of the holonomy along piecewise geodesic
paths. We want to show that the law of the holonomy along arbitrary families of paths with
finite self-intersection is what we expect it to be, namely that given by the discrete theory. The
goal of this section is to prove the following proposition:

Proposition 2.7.1 Let Γ = {a1 , . . . , ar } be a graph on M such that L1 , . . . Lq ∈ Γ∗ . For any
function f continuous on GΓ , we have:
                                                              Z
                                  Ef (Ha1 , . . . , Har ) =        f dPβΓ .
                                                              GΓ

    A very important consequence of this result is the independence of the construction with
respect to the Riemannian metric:

Corollary 2.7.2 The law of the family (Hc )c∈P M does not depend on the choice of the Rieman-
nian metric that was used throughout the construction.

    Proof. Consider two families of variables obtained with two different choices of metric. By
the preceding proposition, these families have the same law on the set of paths that are piecewise
geodesic for, say, the first metric. By proposition 2.6.5, both families are continuous in a sense
that is strong enough to guarantee that their laws coincide on the whole set P M .

    In order to prove the proposition 2.7.1, we need a technical result about the approximation
of graphs by piecewise geodesic graphs. Before to state this result, let us make some remarks
about the edges and faces in a graph in M .
    Recall that a path and hence an edge must by definition have non-zero derivatives at its end
points. This avoids pathological behaviours. For example, consider all edges that share a given
vertex of a graph and a small geodesic circle centered at this vertex. If the radius of this circle
is small enough, each edge cuts it only once, and the order of the intersection points, which does
not depend on the radius of the circle, defines a cyclic order on the set of these edges.
    Now, consider two edges that are adjacent for this order. They bound at least one common
face. Thus, if M is oriented, a couple of adjacent edges determines a face of the graph (see
fig. 2.3). Conversely, given a face, any two consecutive edges of the boundary of this face are
adjacent at the vertex that they share, or eventually at both vertices if they share two.

Proposition 2.7.3 Let Γ = {a1 , . . . , ar } be a graph such that L1 , . . . , Lq ∈ Γ∗ . For any ε > 0,
there exists a graph Γε = {α1 , . . . , αr } with piecewise geodesic edges such that:
    1. Γε and Γ have the same vertices,
    2. For each i = 1, . . . , r, d1 (αi , ai ) < ε,
    3. L1 , . . . , Lq ∈ Γ∗ε ,
    4. For each i = 1, . . . , r, ai and αi are in the same connected component of the complementary
of the unions of the images of the Li ’s.
Let us denote by α : Γ∗ −→ Γ∗ε the multiplicative map that sends ai to αi . It is possible to
construct Γε in such a way that this map induces a one-to-one correspondence still denoted by
α : F(Γ) −→ F(Γε ) such that ∂α(F ) = α(∂F ) and σ(F − ∂F ) < ε, where − denotes the
symmetric difference.
42                                      CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE


                                                       F
                                            a2
                                                           a1


                                                  a3


                     Figure 2.3: The face determined by two adjacent edges.


     Proof. The property 4 is a consequence of 2 and 3. Indeed, if ai is in a given connected
component, αi meets this component if ε is small enough, by 2. But αi could only exit this
component by crossing Li at a point which is not an end point of αi , which is impossible by 3
and by the definition of graphs.
     Let V(Γ) = {s1 , . . . , sp } denote the set of vertices of Γ. Let r be a positive real number that
”localizes the vertices of Γ”, i.e. small enough to satisfy the following properties:
     1. The balls B(si , r) are pairwise disjoint.
     2. For every pair (ai , sj ) with ai ∈ Γ and sj an end point of ai , ai meets only once and
transversally any circle centered at sj and of radius smaller than r. Moreover, the length of the
portion of ai in the corresponding ball is smaller than ε/16.
     3. For any pair (ai , sj ) where sj is not an end point of ai , ai does not meet the ball B(sj , r).
     4. The sum of the surfaces of the ball B(si , r) is smaller than ε/2.
     5. r < ε/16 and r < RM , where RM is the convexity radius of M .
All properties remain true for r ′ < r once they are true for r, so that it is not a problem to get
them simultaneously.
     Let t be a positive real number such that σ({d(·, Γ) < t}) < 2ε . Let a       bi denote the portion of
ai outside the disks of radius r around its end points. Let δ be the smallest distance between the
images of two distinct a   bi . For each i, let γi be an injective piecewise geodesic path with the same
end points as a bi , such that d1 (γi , abi ) < inf(ε/4, δ/2, t) and that never meets the balls B(sj , r),
except at its ends. This last condition can be obtained because ai cuts B(sj , r) transversally:
in a neighbourhood of each end point of a          bi , there is a half-tube around abi that does not meet
B(sj , r). It is possible around each end point of a          bi to construct γi inside this half-tube. By
definition of δ, the γi ’s are disjoint.
     Now define αi for each i such that ai is not piecewise geodesic as the concatenation of the
minimizing geodesic from ai (0) to a        bi (0), of γi and of the minimizing geodesic from a     bi (1) to
ai (1) (see fig. 2.4). Assumption 5 ensures that these minimizing geodesics are well defined.
Assumptions 2 and 5 imply that d1 (αi , ai ) < ε. The edges of the decompositions of the Li ’s are
already piecewise geodesic. Hence we only rename them, setting αi = ai .
     The αi ’s are edges. Moreover, they were constructed in such a way that they meet only at
their ends: we already noticed that they do not meet outside the balls around the vertices of
Γ, and they cannot meet more than once inside these balls according to the local properties of
geodesics. Thus, the graph Γε = {α1 , . . . , αr } exists and has the same vertices as Γ.
     We just proved that properties 1 and 2. Property 3 is true because we kept the edges
corresponding to the Li ’s and property 4 follows, according to the remark made at the beginning
2.7. LAW OF THE RANDOM HOLONOMY                                                                                            43


                              B(s1 , r)                               a1

                                                                  α1




                                                α3                          α2
                                                                                            a2
                                          a3




                        Figure 2.4: Definition of the edges of the graph Γε .

of the proof. It remains to prove the last part of the statement.
    Consider edges of Γ that share a given vertex. They are given a cyclic order. By definition,
the corresponding αi ’s cut the circle of radius r around this vertex in the same order, so that
the multiplicative application α : Γ∗ → Γ∗g defined by α(ai ) = αi preserves the cyclic order at
each vertex.
    Given a pair of edges of Γ that determine the face F , the pair of corresponding edges of Γε is
a pair of adjacent edges that determine a face of Γε . This face does not depend on the particular
choice of the edges that represent F and we denote it by α(F ). By construction, we have the
relation ∂(α(F )) = α(∂F ).
    The symmetric difference of F and α(F ) is contained in the reunion of the balls B(sj , r) and
the sets {d(·, a
               bi ) < t}. By assumption 4) and by definition of t, we know that the total volume
of these sets is smaller than ε. Thus, vol(F − ∂F ) < ε. Moreover, this inequality characterizes
α(F ) among the faces of Γε that have α(∂F ) as boundary, if there is more than one, provided
σ(M ) is greater than 2ε.

    Proof of 2.7.1. For each integer n, the preceding proposition gives a graph Γ 1 =

2.8       Surfaces with boundary
At the beginning of this chapter, we restricted ourselves for technical reasons to surfaces without
boundary. In this section, we will extend the construction of the random holonomy to the case
of surfaces with boundary.

2.8.1      Natural law of the holonomy along the boundary
Let (M, σ) be a surface with a boundary ∂M = N1 ∪. . .∪Np . In order to construct the holonomy
along the paths of M , we shall embed M in a minimal closure and use the construction described
in the preceding sections. But if we want this procedure to give a result independent of the
closure of M , and we do, it is necessary to condition the holonomy along every component of
∂M . If this was not our first intention, say if we expected only to impose the holonomy along
N1 , to be equal to x for example, we need to know the natural law of the holonomy along the
whole boundary under P (x). Then, we will artificially impose this natural law when working
on the closure of M . We begin by defining this natural law.
     Let L1 , . . . , Lq be disjoint simple loops on M whose image is included in the interior of M .
Let N1 , . . . , Nk be the components of ∂M along which we want to impose the holonomy. Let
x1 , . . . , xk , y1 , . . . , yq be elements of G. The k first elements correspond to the components of
∂M , the q others to the interior loops.

Proposition 2.8.1 Let Γ be a graph on M such that L1 , . . . , Lq ∈ Γ∗ . The law of the random
variable (hN1 , . . . , hNp , hL1 , . . . , hLq ) defined on the probability space (GΓ , P (x1 , . . . , xk , y1 , . . . , yq ))
does not depend on Γ. We will denote it by β(x1 , . . . , xk , y1 , . . . , yq ).


Lemma 2.8.2 The propositions 2.7.3 and 2.7.4 hold on surfaces with boundary.

    Proof. Note that 2.7.3 implies 2.7.4, using the computation done at the end of its proof.
Thus, it is sufficient to prove that 2.7.3 holds. For this, embed M in a minimal closure M1 . A
graph Γ on M induces a graph on M1 , which can be approximated by piecewise geodesic graphs.
If each component of ∂M is the image of one of the Li ’s, then the property (4) says exactly that
2.8. SURFACES WITH BOUNDARY                                                                                                                         45

the approximating graphs stay inside M .

     Proofof proposition 2.8.1. Let us endow M with a Riemannian metric for which N1 , . . . , Np ,
L1 , . . . , Lq are geodesics. The law of (hN1 , . . . , hNp , hL1 , . . . , hLq ) does not depend on Γ provided
it is piecewise geodesic, by invariance by subdivision.
     Now consider an arbitrary graph Γ. According to the preceding lemma, we can approximate
it by piecewise geodesic graphs, for which the law we are interested in is always the same. The
convergence of the joint law of the holonomy along all edges proves the result.

    The lemma 1.5.2 gives us an expression of β(x1 , . . . , xk , y1 , . . . , yq ). We state it here again.

Lemma 2.8.3 The following equality between measures on Gp+q holds:

          β(x1 , . . . , xk , y1 , . . . , yq ) =
                                    Z(x1 , . . . , xk , x′k+1 , . . . , x′p , y1 , . . . , yq ) ′

                                               Z(x1 , . . . , xk , y1 , . . . , yq )

    In the particular case where we do not want to condition the measure at all, the last expres-
sion is still true, with the convention that a conditional partition function without parameters
is equal to 1.

2.9       Summary of the properties of the random holonomy
2.9.1      Existence, unicity in law and main properties
Let us summarize what has been done in this chapter. We started with a surface (M, σ), with
or without boundary. We choosed on M disjoint simple loops L1 , . . . , Lq , whose image is either
a boundary component of M or contained in the interior of M . We picked q elements x1 , . . . , xq
in G. We almost proved the following theorem:

Theorem 2.9.1 There exists a probabilty space (Ω, A, P (x1 , . . . , xq )) and a family of G-valued
random variables (Hc )c∈P M on this space, such that:
    1. For any graph Γ = {a1 , . . . , ar } on M such that L1 , . . . , Lq ∈ Γ∗ , the law of (Ha1 , . . . , Har )
is the discrete Yang-Mills measure P Γ (x1 , . . . , xq ) on GΓ .
                                                                                      1                                                 d
     2. For any path c of P M and any sequence (cn )n≥0 of paths of P M such that cn −→ c with
                                                                                                                                      n→∞
                                  P              d
fixed end points, we have Hcn −→     Hc .
                                n→∞
The law of this family of random variables is uniquely defined by these two properties. Moreover,
it has the following properties:
    3. If c1 and c2 are paths that can be concatenated to form c1 c2 , then Hc1 c2 = Hc2 Hc1 a.s.
    4. If ϕ : M −→ M is a diffeomorphism such that ϕ∗ σ = σ, then ϕ induces a permutation of
the set of paths P M and the families (Hc )c∈P M and (Hϕ(c) )c∈P M have the same law.
2.10. YANG-MILLS MEASURE                                                                                             47

    Proof. We already proved the existence of the family. When M has a boundary, the
probability space is that associated with a minimal closure of M . Let us prove the uniqueness
in law. This law is a probability measure on the set F(P M, G) endowed with the σ-algebra
generated by cylinder sets. So it is characterized by its finite-dimensional marginals. Since
any family of paths can be approximated by families of paths in graphs, for example piecewise
geodesic paths for some metric, the law of the random holonomy along an arbitrary finite family
of paths is determined by properties (1) and (2).
    Property (3) was already proved in proposition 2.6.4 for closed surfaces. For surfaces with
boundary, the construction by restriction of the random holonomy on a minimal closure obviously
preserves the multiplicativity.
    Property (4) was proved at the discrete level in proposition 1.7.1. Since the law of the whole
family is determined by discrete laws, it is also true is the continuous setting.

    Given (M, σ), L1 , . . . , Lq , x1 , . . . , xq , the law whose existence and uniqueness is stated by
this theorem is a measure on (F(LM, G), C), where C is the σ-algebra generated by the cylinder
sets. We shall denote this measure by µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ), or just µ0 if q = 0. We keep the notation
(Hc )c∈P M for the canonical process on the space (F(LM, G), C).

2.9.2     Disintegration formula
Consider a surface (M, σ). Recall from proposition 1.5.3 that the conditional discrete measures
constitute a disintegration of the free discrete Yang-Mills measure. We want to extend this
result to the continuous setting. As usual, L1 , . . . , Lq are loops on M .

Proposition 2.9.2 The map (x1 , . . . , xq ) 7→ µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ) provides a disintegration of the mea-
sure µ0 on (F(P M, G), C) with respect to the random variable (HL1 , . . . , HLq ).

   Proof. By construction, (HL1 , . . . , HLq ) = (x1 , . . . , xq ) µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq )-a.s. Let (c1 , . . . , cn )
be a family of paths of P M . We need to prove that, for any function f continuous on Gq ,
                                               Z
                Eµ0 f (Hc1 , . . . , Hcq ) =        Eµ0 (x1 ,...,xq ) f (Hc1 , . . . , Hcq ) dη(x1 , . . . , xq ),
                                               Gq

where η is the law of (HL1 , . . . , HLq ) under µ0 . We already know that this result is true if
c1 , . . . , cn are paths in a graph. If they are not, we can approximate them in the d1 -topology
by paths in graphs so that both expectations appearing in the formula converge. Since G is
compact, f is bounded and the dominated convergence theorem applies.



2.10       Yang-Mills measure
2.10.1      Definition of the Yang-Mills measure
In this paragraph, we will explain why and how the measure µ0 defined in the preceding sec-
tion still has to be transformed in order to become something that might be called Yang-Mills
measure.
    According to the formal description, Yang-Mills measure should be a measure on the quotient
space A/J of connections modulo gauge transformations. But an element of this space does
48                                           CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE

not determine a holonomy along each path on M that could be intrinsically represented by an
element of G. Indeed, the holonomy along an open path c, i.e. such that c(0) 6= c(1), can be
transformed into any other G-equivariant diffeomorphism of the fiber over c(0) into the fiber over
c(1) by an appropriate gauge transformation. The fact that the law of the random holonomy
along an edge is always uniform on G could be thought of as a reflect of this geometric property.
This is why we will restrict to the set LM of loops on M instead of P M . Thus we will consider
the family (Hl )l∈LM whose law is a probability measure on (F(LM, G), C), where we keep the
notation C for the σ-algebra generated by the cylinders.
    But it is still not true that an element of A/J determines an element of G as holonomy
along each loop. Gauge transformations act by conjugation on the holonomy along loops. More
precisely, they conjugate in the same way the holonomies along loops based at the same point.
Let us denote by Ad the diagonal action of G on Gn defined by:

                               Ad(g)(g1 , . . . , gn ) = (Ad(g)g1 , . . . , Ad(g)gn ).

Orbits of this action will be called joint conjugacy classes and the joint class of (g1 , . . . , gn ) will
be denoted by [g1 , . . . , gn ]. We can reformulate our observation by saying that an element of
A/J determines the the joint conjugacy class of the holonomy along all loops based at the same
point. Sengupta proved the converse of this statement (prop. 2.1.2 in [Se1]):

Proposition 2.10.1 ([Se1]) Let ω1 and ω2 be two connections on M . Let m0 be a point on M .
Suppose that along any finite family of loops l1 , . . . , ln based at m0 , the joint conjugacy classes
of the holonomies defined by ω1 and ω2 are equal. Then ω1 and ω2 belong to the same class in
A/J .

   Let the group F(M, G) act on F(LM, G) in the following way: if j ∈ F(M, G), f ∈
F(LM, G) and l ∈ LM , set

                                       (j · f )(l) = j(l(0))−1 f (l)j(l(0)).

This action extends the action of a discrete gauge transformation. We can summarize our
observations as follows:

Proposition 2.10.2 The holonomy allows to define an injective map

                                       A/J −→ F(LM, G)/F(M, G).

    This result says that the quotient space F(LM, G)/F(M, G) can be viewed as an extension
of the space of connections modulo gauge transformations. We want to define the Yang-Mills
measure on this space. To begin with, we must define a convenient σ-algebra.
    There is a set of natural functions on the quotient space: given n loops l1 , . . . , ln based at the
same point, the joint class [Hl1 , . . . , Hln ] is a well-defined function that we denote by Hl1 ,...,ln .
We will consider the σ-algebra A generated by the set of these functions. Of course, we want to
be able to consider random variables associated with families of loops that are not based at the
same point. We claim that the σ-algebra A allows to do this. Indeed, let l1 , . . . , ln be a family
of loops that we rewrite (l1 , . . . , li1 ), . . . , (lip +1 , . . . , ln ), putting together the loops based at the
same points. Then we can define the variable Hl1 ,...,ln by

                                    Hl1 ,...,ln = (Hl1 ,...,li1 , . . . , Hlip +1 ,...,ln )
2.10. YANG-MILLS MEASURE                                                                                      49

and this random variable is measurable with respect to A. Remark that A may also be seen as
a σ-algebra on F(LM, G), invariant by the action of F(M, G), since the functions Hl1 ,...,lq are
also naturally defined on this space. Another natural choice for A would have been to consider
the F(M, G)-invariant sets of the cylinder σ-algebra C. We shall discuss this point at the end
of this section.

Proposition 2.10.3 Let (M, σ) be a surface. Let L1 , . . . , Lq be disjoint simple loops on M
whose image is either a component of the boundary of M or contained in the interior of M . Let
(x1 , . . . , xq ) be an element of Gq . The restriction of µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ) to A depends on each xi only
through its conjugacy class.

    Proof. The point is to understand how µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ) is transformed under the action of
F(M, G). Similarly to what we proved in 1.5.4, if j is an element of F(M, G), then, setting
yi = j(Li (0)), we have
                               j∗ P (x1 , . . . , xq ) = P (y1−1 x1 y1 , . . . , yq−1 xq yq ).
Indeed, we already know that this equality holds when we evaluate these measures against
functions of the holonomy along paths in a graph, and we extend it to general measurable
functions by the usual approximation scheme.
   Thus, the µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq )-measure of sets invariant under the action of F(M, G) depends only
on the conjugacy classes [x1 ], . . . , [xq ].

    We denote by ti the conjugacy class of each xi .

Definition 2.10.4 We call Yang-Mills measure on M and denote by µ the image measure
of µ0 on the quotient space (F(LM, G)/F(M, G), A), or equivalently the restriction of µ0 to
(F(LM, G), A).
    Similarly, we call conditional Yang-Mills measure with respect to L1 , . . . , Lq and we denote by
µ(t1 , . . . , tq ) the image measure of µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ) on the quotient space (F(LM, G)/F(M, G), A),
or equivalently the restriction of µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ) to (F(LM, G), A).

    The first point of view keeps track of the quotient structure of the space A/J . Nevertheless,
the second will often be technically more convenient.

Proposition 2.10.5 The map (t1 , . . . , tq ) 7→ µ(t1 , . . . , tq ) defined on (G/ Ad)q provides a dis-
integration of the measure µ with respect to the random variable HL1 , . . . , HLq .

   Note that since the Li ’s are not based at the same point, the variables HL1 ,...,Lq and
(HL1 , . . . , HLq ) are equal.

     Proof. Let (t1 , . . . , tq ) be an element of (G/ Ad)q and (x1 , . . . , xq ) ∈ Gn be such that
[xi ] = ti for each i. Then ([HL1 ], . . . , [HLq ]) = ([x1 ], . . . , [xq ]) µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq )-a.s., so that
                            (HL1 , . . . , HLq ) = (t1 , . . . , tq ) µ(t1 , . . . , tq ) − a.s.
By 1.5.2, we know that the law of (HL1 , . . . , HLq ) under µ0 is Z −1 Z(x1 , . . . , xq ) dx1 . . . dxq . We
also proved in 2.9.2 that µ0 is disintegrated by the µ0 (x1 , . . . , xq ), so that

                                  Z       Gq
50                                            CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE

If we evaluate these measures on sets of A and use the invariance by conjugation of the condi-

                                      Z

where dt is the image measure on G/ Ad of the Haar measure. The last equality restricted to

2.10.2      Regularity properties
In order to study Yang-Mills measure, we need to say more about the set of joint conjugacy
classes Gn / Ad. We regard it as a set of compact subsets of Gn and endow it with the Hausdorff
distance, defined in general between two compact sets by

                      d(K1 , K2 ) = sup( sup          inf d(k1 , k2 ), sup         inf d(k1 , k2 )).
                                             k1 ∈K1 k2 ∈K2               k2 ∈K2 k1 ∈K1




Lemma 2.10.6 The canonical projection Gn −→ Gn / Ad is 1-Lipchitz.

     Proof. Let (g1 , . . . , gn ) and (h1 , . . . , hn ) be two elements of Gn .

           d([g1 , . . . , gn ], [h1 , . . . , hn ]) = sup inf d(Ad(g)(g1 , . . . gn ), Ad(h)(h1 , . . . , hn ))
                                                    g∈G h∈G
                                               =    inf d((g1 , . . . , gn ), Ad(h)(h1 , . . . , hn ))
                                                    h∈G
                                               ≤ d((g1 , . . . , gn ), (h1 , . . . , hn )).


     As before, this distance on Gn / Ad allows to define the distance

                                               dP (X, Y ) = Ed(X, Y )

between Gn / Ad-valued random variables defined on the same probability space. The regularity
property 2.6.5 of the random holonomy becomes the following regularity property for the Yang-
Mills measure:

2.10.3     Remarkable subfamilies of random variables
We study two special subfamilies of random variables defined on (F(LM, G)/F(M, G), A, µ),
using the results proved in the preceding paragraphs.
    We begin by the family (Hl )l∈LM . Each variable is G/ Ad-valued and this family satisfies a
very nice regularity property:

Proposition 2.10.8 Let l be in LM and (ln )n≥0 be a sequence of loops that converges to l.
          P d
Then Hln −→ Hl .

    This is the only situation where we can forget about end points. Unfortunately, this family
does not generate A, since it does not contain any information about joint conjugacy classes.
    Now fix a point m ∈ M and consider the set Lm M of loops based at m. We are interested
in the family (Hl1 ,...,ln )li ∈Lm M . It has the same property as that stated in 2.10.7, the condition
on end points being always satisfied. What is interesting here is the following fact:

Proposition 2.10.9 The family (Hl1 ,...,ln )li ∈Lm M generates the σ-algebra A.

    Proof. Let l1 , . . . , ln be n loops on M based at a point m1 . Let c be a path joining m to
m1 . Then the equality
                                       Hcl1 c−1 ,...,cln c−1 = Hl1 ,...,ln
proves that it is always possible to get back to loops based at m.

      This subfamily satisfies also a multiplicativity property. Indeed, the joint conjugacy class
of some elements of G determines the joint class of all products of these elements. For ex-
ample, there is a well defined map from Gn / Ad to Gn−1 / Ad that sends [g1 , . . . , gn−1 , gn ] to
[g1 , . . . , gn−2 , gn gn−1 ]. The multiplicativity can be expressed by saying that for any l1 , . . . , ln ∈
Lm M , this maps sends almost surely Hl1 ,...,ln to Hl1 ,...,ln−1 ln .
52                                    CHAPTER 2. CONTINUOUS YANG-MILLS MEASURE

Remark.
Let us discuss the definition of the σ-algebra A. For this, we consider the Yang-Mills measure
an invariant measure on the space F(Lm M, G), because in this setting, the action of the gauge
group is that of the finite dimensional group G. We use this fact below in order to integrate
functions over the orbits of this action.
    We could have made another natural choice of an invariant σ-algebra on the space F(Lm M, G),
namely that of invariant sets of the cylinder σ-algebra C on F(Lm M, G). Let us denote by CI
this σ-algebra. It is clear that A ⊂ CI and it is very likely that this inclusion is in fact an
equality. We prove that the completions Ae and CfI with respect to µM (x) are equal.
    We use the separability of Lm M proved in the next lemma.

Lemma 2.10.10 Let M be a surface and m a point of M . The loop space Lm M endowed with
the d1 -topology is separable.

    Proof. We construct a countable dense subset of Lm M . The first point is that M itself is
separable. Choose a countable dense subset Π ⊂ M containing m. Endow M with a Riemannian
metric such that ∂M is geodesic if it is non empty. Let RM be the convexity radius of M : two
points at distance smaller than RM are joined by a unique minimizing geodesic. Define Λ to
be the set of loops obtained by concatenation of a finite number of geodesic segments joining
two points of Π at distance smaller than RM . The set Λ is countable because it is equipotent
to a subset of finite sequences of Π. We claim that it is dense in Lm M . Indeed, any geodesic
segment of length smaller than RM can be approximated by segments joining points of Π,
since a small piece of geodesic depends continuously on its end points. Thus, the d1 -closure of Λ
contains the set of piecewise geodesic loops and we already know that this set is dense in Lm M .

     Let Λ ⊂ Lm M be a countable dense subset. Let AΛ denote σ(Hλ1 ,...,λn ∈ Λ). Let C Λ denote
σ(Hλ , λ ∈ Λ) and (C Λ )I denote the invariant sets of this σ-algebra. It is clear that AΛ ⊂ (C Λ )I .
It is also clear that AΛ is a separable σ-algebra. Finally, one easily checks that any atom of AΛ is
contained in an atom of (C Λ )I . Thus, Blackwell’s theorem implies (see [DM]) that AΛ = (C Λ )I .
     We use this equality to prove the inclusion CI ⊂ A,  e which implies the result. The point is
that the continuity in probability of the map l 7→ Hl and the density of Λ in Lm M imply that
CfΛ contains C and that A  g Λ contains A. This last inclusion implies the equality Ag      e Thus,
                                                                                       Λ = A.
                                     g Λ
it is sufficient to prove that CI ⊂ A . Let f be a CI -measurable function. As a C-measurable
function, it is µM (x)-almost surely the limit of a sequence (fn ) of C Λ -measurable functions. Let
us integrate these functions fn over the orbits of the action of G on F(Lm M, G), using the Haar
measure on G. We get a sequence of (C Λ )I -measurable functions still converging to f . Thus, f
is measurable with respect to the completion (Cg   Λ) = A
                                                      I
                                                          g  Λ and we get the result.
Chapter 3

Abelian theory

In this chapter, we continue the investigation of the case G = U (1) started in section 1.9. Recall
that we had reconstructed the random holonomy along loops homologous to zero in a graph,
using a white noise on M (see proposition 1.9.10). We extend now this reconstruction to all
cycles of M , using the unicity properties of the Yang-Mills measure proved in chapter 2.
    Then we show that it is possible to proceed backwards, namely to extract a white noise on
M from the Yang-Mills measure on M , more precisely, using the random holonomy along very
small loops. This makes clear the relationship between the random holonomy and the white
noise in this Abelian case.


3.1     The random holonomy as a white noise functional
As usual, M may have a boundary ∂M = N1 ∪ . . . ∪ Np . Choose elements x1 , . . . , xp in U (1)
and set x = x1 . . . xp or x = 1 if M has no boundary. We denote by CM the set of cycles on M ,
i.e. the set of linear combination of loops with integer coefficients and by C0 Γ the set of cycles
homologous to zero. The family of random variables (Hl )l∈LM , that we used in 2.10 to construct
the Yang-Mills measure µM (x1 , . . . , xp ), extends by multiplicativity to the cycles of CM and
gives rise to a measure on F(CM, U (1)). In this Abelian setting, the action of F(M, U (1)) is
trivial.
     We seek a result similar to 1.9.9, valid for all cycles on M . We begin by defining a family of
random variables using a white noise on M and prove later that it has the law of a Yang-Mills
random holonomy.
     Recall that we proved earlier that the holonomies along a system of loops representing a
basis of H1 (M ; Z) are independent uniform variables on U (1), independent of the holonomies
along loops homologous to zero (see proposition 1.9.4).

3.1.1    Definition of the white noise functional
Recall that g denotes the genus of M . In order to define the double layer potential (see 1.9.6),
we need a Riemannian metric on M , that we choose such that the boundary of M is geodesic.
Let ℓ1 , . . . , ℓ2g be piecewise geodesic loops such that

    The homology class of a cycle c depends continuously on c, even for the distance d∞ . Thus,

multiplicative.



3.1.3    Identification of the random holonomies
We are now able to prove the main theorem of this section:

Theorem 3.1.11 The family of random variables (WHc )c∈CM has the same law as the family
(Hc )c∈CM under µM (x1 , . . . , xp ).

    Proof: According to the unicity statement of the theorem 2.9.1 and to the regularity
property 3.1.9, it is enough to prove the equality of the laws for piecewise geodesic cycles. Let
Γ be a piecewise geodesic graph such that ℓ1 , . . . , ℓ2g ∈ Γ∗ . Recall that these loops generate
the first homology group of a minimal closure of M . Denote by F1 , . . . , Fn the faces of Γ. The
arguments developped in 1.9.1 explain why it is enough to prove the equality of the laws for the
fundamental system (ℓ1 , . . . , ℓ2g , N1 , . . . , Np , ∂F1 , . . . , ∂Fn ).
    On one hand, ℓ⊥                  ⊥
                     1 = . . . = ℓ2g = 0, so that WHℓi = Ui for all i = 1, . . . , 2g. On the other
                       ⊥ = 0 and N ⊥ = N + . . . + N . Thus, σ (N ⊥ ) = 1 and u
hand, N1⊥ = . . . = Np−1                 p           1               p        int p    Np⊥ is equal to
zero so that W0 (uNp⊥ ) = 0. This implies WHNj = xj for all j = 1, . . . , p − 1 and also for j = p.
Finally, we already proved in 1.9.9 that (WH∂F1 , . . . , WH∂Fn ) and (H∂F1 , . . . , H∂Fn ) have the
same law. This terminates the proof, since we know that (H∂F1 , . . . , H∂Fn ) and (Hℓ1 , . . . , Hℓ2g )
are independent under the Yang-Mills measure.

3.2     Small scale structure of the Yang-Mills field
3.2.1    Extraction of a white noise
In the first part of this chapter, we explained how the data of a white noise on M and a bit
more alea allows to reconstruct the Yang-Mills measure. We proceed now backwards: we try to
extract a white noise from the Yang-Mills measure on a surface. In some sense, this amounts to
compute the curvature of a Yang-Mills random connection.
    As usual, (M, σ) is given, as well as elements x1 , . . . , xp of G, associated with the components
of ∂M and x = x1 . . . xp or x = 1 if M has no boundary. We denote by (ΩM , µM (x1 , . . . , xp ))
the space of the Yang-Mills measure on M .
    In order to study the measure at small scale, we construct on M a sequence of partitions in
the following way. Let (Γn )n≥0 be a sequence of graphs on M such that Γn has exactly n faces
denoted by Fj,n , j = 1, . . . , n. We assume that σ(Fj,n ) = σ(M      n
                                                                         )
                                                                           and also that the diameter of
the faces decreases uniformly to 0, i.e. that for any metric on M , supj diam(Fj,n ) −→ 0. We fix
an orientation of M and assume that the boundaries of the Fj,n ’s are oriented with the usual
convention. For each couple (j, n) with n ≥ 0, 1 ≤ j ≤ n, we denote the random variable H∂Fj,n
defined on (ΩM , µM (x1 , . . . , xp )) by Hj,n and see it as a C-valued random variable, identifying
U (1) with {z ∈ C, |z| = 1}.
    For each n ≥ 0, let En denote the space of functions on M constant on each face of Γn .
Set E∞ = ∪n En . The assumption on the diameter of the faces Fj,n imply that any continuous
function on M can be uniformly approximated by functions of E∞ . Thus, E∞ is dense in
L1 (M, σ) and L2 (M, σ) with their respective usual norms.
    In order to define a kind of white noise, we will proceed as for the construction of the standard
Wiener integral. We define a linear form In on each En . Let fn be a function of En and let fj,n
be its value on Fj,n . We set

3.2.2   Meaning of the variable T
As a conclusion for this chapter, we will spend a few lines to suggest a geometric interpretation
for the variable T , whose meaning could seem to be quite mysterious.
    In a deterministic setting with a smooth connection ω, a construction similar to that of the
map f 7→ I(f ) would have given the map:
                                                                   Z
                                                            f 7→        f F (ω),
                                                                    M

where F (ω) is the curvature 2-form of ω. As long as we consider zero-mean functions, the
comparison between this formula and (3.2) is in agreement with the heuristic principle saying
62                                                           CHAPTER 3. ABELIAN THEORY

that the curvature of a Yang-Mills random connection is a white noise, as explained in the
introduction.
    If we take
           R
               the function f identically equal to 1 in the deterministic setting, we get the total
curvature M F (ω) of the fiber bundle P on which ω lives. This quantity is well known to be
independent of ω and to be a topological invariant of P , namely its first Chern class. The
probabilistic counterpart of this total curvature seems then to be I(1) = T , droping out the
imaginary part. This discussion becomes really meaningful when M is closed, because P is not
necessarily trivial. We mentioned at the beginning of the discretization procedure in section
1.3 that we had lost any topological information about the structure of P . If we compute the
“random Chern class” of P at the end of the construction, we find a weighted sum of all possible
Chern classes, with the smallest weights for the most complicated types of bundles. This was
already suggested by Witten [Wi].
    On the other hand, we can change our point of view in the following way: we have an
expression of the random holonomy which depends explicitely on the Chern class of P . So if we
replace T by a deterministic multiple of 2π in the definition 3.1.1, we are able to construct a
random holonomy consistent with any prescribed type of bundle P .


3.3     Square-integrability of the double-layer potential
In this section, we prove the theorem 1.9.11. We claim that it is enough to prove the theorem
on closed surfaces. Indeed, we did not use the square-integrability of the double-layer potential
to prove the results 3.1.4 and 3.1.5, which show that the result on a surface with boundary can
be deduced from the result on a minimal closure of this surface. Thus, we assume that M is
closed.
Proposition 3.3.1 There exists RF > 0 such that for all embedded path c ∈ P M such that
ℓ(c) < 14 RF , the double layer potential uc of c is in L∞ (M ).
    This proposition implies obviously the theorem. It implies even more, namely that the dou-
ble layer potential of any path is in L∞ .

    Proof. The proof relies on three facts. The first one is that we know the divergence near
the diagonal of the Green function in an open subset of R2 . The second one is that, according
to a classical theorem due to Gauss [Ch], any metric on M is locally conformally flat. The third
point is that the Green function is conformally invariant.
    Since M is compact, the second remark implies that there exists a radius RF such that any
geodesic ball of M of radius smaller than RF is conformally flat. Let us choose an embedded
path c of length smaller than RF /4. For each r > 0, we denote by Br the ball B(c(0), r). Since
ℓ(c) is smaller than RF /4, c is contained in B 1 RF . Since the Green function G is smooth outside
                                                2
the diagonal, uc is smooth outside B 1 RF . It is enough now to prove that it is bounded on B 3 RF
                                       2                                                       4
for example. Set r = 43 RF .
    The values of uc inside B r depend only on the restriction of G to B r × B r . On this set,
                             1
G satisfies ∆Gx = δx − σ(M     ) . Our idea is to substract smooth functions to G until we get
something easier to compute than G itself. Denote by G0 the solution of:
                                  (
                                                  1
                                      ∆G0x = − σ(M   )
                                      G0x (y) = Gx (y) ∀y ∈ ∂Br .
3.3. SQUARE-INTEGRABILITY OF THE DOUBLE-LAYER POTENTIAL                                         63

It is a smooth function inside Br . The function G1 defined by G1 = G − G0 satisfies
                                         

                                             G1x (y) = 0 ∀y ∈ ∂Br .

So, G1 is the fundamental solution of ∆ inside Br with Dirichlet boundary conditions. We can
decompose uc (x) for any x in Br according to:
                                     Z               Z
                          uc (x) =       ∗dG0x +          ∗dG1x = u0c (x) + u1c (x).
                                     c                c

The first term is smooth and we are led to study the second. This is where conformally flat
coordinates are useful: we choose a local chart ϕ : U −→ Br , where U is an open subset of R2 ,
such that the pull-back of the metric g of M by ϕ is conformally equivalent to dx2 + dy 2 on
U . The point is that ϕ∗ G1 is not only the fundamental solution of ∆ϕ∗ g with respect to the
measure induced by ϕ∗ g, but also the fundamental solution of ∆0 = ∂x2 + ∂y2 with respect to
the flat metric on U , by conformal invariance of the Green function. This tells us that ϕ∗ G1
               1
diverges like 4π log d(·, ·) on the diagonal. In other words, there exists a smooth function G2 on
                                1
Br × Br such that ϕ∗ G1 = 4π      log d(·, ·) + ϕ∗ G2 .
    What we want is to prove that u1c (x) is bounded inside Br . It is equivalent to prove that
 1
uc ◦ ϕ is bounded on U . But for any y ∈ U ,
                                             Z                 Z
                           u1c ◦ ϕ(y) =           ∗dG1ϕ(y) =                 ∗d(ϕ∗ G1y ).

Note that in this last term, the Hodge operator ∗ is that of the metric ϕ∗ g. It is not the
same operator as that of the flat metric on U . Fortunately, the fact that these two metrics are
conformally equivalent implies that their Hodge operators are pointwise proportionnal, i.e. one
is deduced from the other by theR multiplication by a positive bounded smooth function. Thus,
it is sufficient to prove that y 7→ ϕ−1 (c) ∗dϕ∗ G1y is bounded, the Hodge operator being now that
of the flat metric. As already noticed, we can remove a smooth part of G1 and keep only the
part                                        Z
                                        1
                                                    ∗d log d(y, ·).
                                       4π ϕ−1 (c)
A short computation shows that 1/4π ∗ d log d(y, ·) is nothing but 1/2π times the angle form dθ
of the polar coordinates centered at y. This allows us to estimate very easily the integral of this
form along a path.
    For example, we know that the integral of this form along a simple loop is bounded by 1.
On the other hand, it is obvious and easy to prove by a direct computation that the integral of
this form along a straight segment is bounded by 1/2.
    Consider the path ϕ−1 (c). It is injective, hence it is possible to transform it into a simple
loop by concatenating it with a finite number of segments. So, we can make the function that
we want to estimate to be bounded by adding to it a finite number of bounded functions. This
gives the result.
Chapter 4

Small scale structure in the
semi-simple case

The theorem 3.1.11 shows that it is possible to construct the Yang-Mills measure in a short and
quite pleasant way when G = U (1), using a white noise on M as main ingredient. Is it possible
to do something similar in general? The works of Sengupta and Driver [Dr2, Se1, Se2] lead to
an ambiguous answer to that question. Indeed, in these works, the authors have constructed
random holonomies, starting from a Lie algebra-valued white noise on M . Nevertheless, the
family of loops along which they are able to define the holonomy is strongly dependent of a
particular choice of coordinates on M , as we explained in the introduction. We think that this
is more than a simple technical problem. Although there might exist some generalization of
the construction made in section 3.1, we will show that a white noise is probably not the right
object to start with.
    Our idea is the following. Was it possible to realize the random holonomy using a white
noise, it would be possible to find a lot of information by looking at the random holonomy at
small scale, i.e. along very small loops. For example, the theorem 3.2.1 basically says that when
G = U (1), almost all the information about the holonomy along homologically trivial loops
is available at infinitesimally small scale. We prove that, when G is semi-simple, there is no
information at all available at infinitesimally small scale, at least when one looks at it in the
same way that we did in the Abelian case.


4.1     Statement of a zero-one law
We begin by stating the main result. The surface is (M, σ) as usual. We assume that G is a
compact connected semi-simple Lie group, for example SU (2). We choose x1 , . . . , xp in G and
consider the probability space (ΩM , µM (x1 , . . . , xp )).
    Let L be a simple loop on M which is the boundary of an open set D diffeomorphic to a
disk. For each n ≥ 0, consider a graph on D which has exactly n faces F1,n , . . . , Fn,n such that
σ(Fi,n ) = σ(D)
             n for each i. This is very similar to the situation described in the section 3.2.
    Was G Abelian, we would have the equality of cycles L = ∂F1,n + . . . + ∂Fn,n , provided
orientations are well chosen. This would imply HL = H∂F1,n . . . H∂Fn,n and for any function f
continuous on G/ Ad = G,
                             E[f (HL )|H∂F1,n , . . . , H∂Fn,n ] = f (HL ).

                                                  65
66                CHAPTER 4. SMALL SCALE STRUCTURE IN THE SEMI-SIMPLE CASE

     When G is semi-simple, the situtation is the opposite.

Theorem 4.1.1 For any function f continuous on G/ Ad, the following convergence holds:

                                                                       L2

    Since we are dealing with functions on G/ Ad, it is natural to use the theory of characters
on G. We give a very short account of the results that we will use. For a detailed presentation
of the subject, see for example [Br, Si].

4.2.2      Characters of a semi-simple Lie group
A representation of G is a smooth morphism of groups ρ from G into the linear group of some
Cn . The integer n is the dimension of ρ. Since G is compact, we may always assume that
ρ(G) is included in the unitary group. A representation is said to be irreducible if there are
no subspaces of Cn invariant by all ρ(g) except Cn and 0. Two representations ρ1 and ρ2
of same dimension n are said to be equivalent if there exists a linear isomorphism φ of Cn
such that ρ1 ◦ φ = φ ◦ ρ2 . . The character of ρ is the C-valued function χρ defined on G by
χρ (g) = tr ρ(g). Two equivalent representations have the same character. In fact, this is also a
sufficient condition of equivalence. The usual properties of the trace imply that it is a central
68              CHAPTER 4. SMALL SCALE STRUCTURE IN THE SEMI-SIMPLE CASE

function on G, that is, χρ (g) depends only on the conjugacy class of g. Since all representations
are unitary, the relation χρ (g−1 ) = χρ (g)∗ holds, the star denoting the complex conjugation.
Note that χρ (1) = dim ρ. The main theorem is the following:
Theorem 4.2.2 (Peter-Weyl theorem) The set of characters of all equivalence classes of
irreducible representations of G is an orthonormal basis of the space of central square-integrable
functions on (G, dg). Moreover, the algebra generated by this set is dense in the set of continuous
C-valued central functions on G endowed with the uniform norm.
    According to this theorem, any continuous function can be approximated by linear combi-
nations of products of characters. But it is a fact that such combinations can always be written
as linear combinations of characters of irreducible representations. Thus it is sufficient to prove
our theorem when f is the character of an irreducible representation.
    Characters satisfy orthogonality relations that give rise to useful formulas. We will mainly
use two of them.
Proposition 4.2.3 For any x, y, z ∈ G and for any irreducible representation α of G,
                        Z
                                                  1
                            χα (xyx−1 z) dx =          χα (x)χα (y),                             (4.1)
                                                dim  α
                       Z G
                                                    1
                            χα ([a, b]x) dadb =           χα (x).                                (4.2)
                        G 2                     (dim  α)2
   Let us endow G with its biinvariant metric normalized to have total volume equal to 1. This
metric gives rise to a Laplace operator on G. A remarkable property of the characters is that
they are eigenfunctions for this operator. More precisely, for any irreducible representation α,
there exists a positive real number c2 (α) such that
                                         ∆χα = −c2 (α)χα .
   A nice application of these properties is the computation of the character expansion of the
heat kernel on G. Let us denote by Gb the set of classes of irreducible representations of G.

Proposition 4.2.4 The following equality holds in L2 (G, dg) and also pointwise on G:

4.2.4      Zero-one law on the plane
In order to prove proposition 4.2.5, we begin with the following result, which can be seen as a
reformulation of the zero-one law when the manifold M is the plane R2 .

Proposition 4.2.6 Let (Btn )t∈R+ ,n≥0 be a sequence of independent Brownian motions on G.
For any irreducible representation β of G and any positive real number T , the following conver-
gence holds in distribution:

                                               Yn

    This result is really the center of the whole proof of the theorem. It is the place where the
fact that G is semi-simple will be used, in the following way. Given a representation ρ of G, the
differential at 1 of ρ is a linear map from T1 G, the Lie algebra of G, into L(Cn ). The following
statement is proved in Bourbaki (Lie, chap. I, § 6, No 2, corollary of the th. 1) [B2]:

Proposition 4.2.7 Let G be a semi-simple group and ρ a representation of G of dimension n.
Then
                                  d1 ρ(T1 G) ⊂ sln (C),
where sln (C) denotes the set of endomorphisms of Cn whose trace is equal to zero.

Chapter 5

Surgery of surfaces

5.1     Markov property of the Yang-Mills field
Consider a surface M which is cut into two pieces M1 and M2 . Our aim in the first part of this
chapter is to understand the relationships between the Yang-Mills measures on M1 , M2 and M .
We already met a question of this kind when we constructed the random holonomy on a surface
with boundary starting from that on a minimal closure of this surface and this led us to prove
in a particular case the Markov property which is the object of the theorem 5.1.1.
    Let M1 and M2 be two oriented surfaces with boundary such that ∂M1 and ∂M2 have at
least p boundary components, where p > 0 is arbitrary. Pick p components N1 , . . . , Np on
∂M1 and p others N1′ , . . . , Np′ on ∂M2 . For each i = 1, . . . , p, consider an orientation-reversing
diffeomorphism ψi : Ni −→ Ni′ and call M the result of the gluing of M1 and M2 along ψ1 , . . . , ψp .
Denote by L1 , . . . , Lp p loops on M whose images are the components of the common boundary
of M1 and M2 , oriented as boundary components of M1 .
    For the statement of the theorem 5.1.1, we consider the Yang-Mills measures on M , M1 and
M2 measures on the spaces F(LM, G), F(LM1 , G) and F(LM2 , G) endowed with the σ-algebras
A, A1 and A2 generated by the variables of the form Hl1 ,...,ln .
    There are two natural sub-σ-algebras on the space (F(LM, G), A), namely Aei = σ(Hl1 ,...,ln , lk ∈
LMi ), i = 1, 2. For any i = 1, 2, a function fi on F(LMi , G) gives rise to a function fei on
F(LM, G) and it is equivalent to say that fi is Ai -measurable or to say that fei is Aei - measur-
able, so that we identify Ai and Aei , as well as fi and fei .

Theorem 5.1.1 The σ-algebras A1 and A2 are independent on F(LM, G) under µM condition-
ally to the random variable (HL1 , . . . , HLp ). Moreover, let f1 and f2 be two measurable functions
on (F(LM1 , G), A1 ) and (F(LM2 , G), A2 ) respectively. Then the product f1 f2 can be seen as
a A-measurable function on F(LM, G) and for any t1 , . . . , tp ∈ G/ Ad, the following equality
holds:
                 µM (t1 , . . . , tp )(f1 f2 ) = µM1 (t1 , . . . , tp )(f1 )µM2 (t−1           −1
                                                                                  1 , . . . , tp )(f2 ). (5.1)
Finally, these properties remain true if we condition further the Yang-Mills measures with respect
to the random holonomy along some boundary components or some interior loops on M1 or M2 .

   This theorem says two things. It says that the random holonomy on M1 is independent of
that on M2 conditionally to the holonomy along the common boundary of M1 and M2 and it

                                                     75
76                                                                       CHAPTER 5. SURGERY OF SURFACES

also says that the restriction to Ai of the measure µM (t1 , . . . , tp ) is equal via the identification
mentioned above to the measure µMi (t1 , . . . , tp ), that is, the Yang-Mills measure on Mi .
    We prove first a discrete result, which is due, in a slightly different form, to Becker and
Sengupta [BS].

Proposition 5.1.2 Let Γ be a graph on M . Let Γ1 and Γ2 be the graphs on M1 and M2 induced
by Γ. Let f be a continuous function on GΓ1 × GΓ2 invariant by both the gauge transformations
on Γ1 and Γ2 . Then f gives rise to a gauge-invariant function on GΓ , still denoted by f and for
any x ∈ Gp ,      Z                                      Z
                                      f dP Γ1 (y1 , x)dP Γ2 (x−1 , y2 ) =                 f dP Γ (y1 , x, y2 ),

    This depends on the fact that f1 D Γ1 is a gauge-invariant function on GΓ1 and on the biin-
variance of the Haar measure. Indeed, consider a gauge transformation j on Γ1 , which is equal
to 1, except on vertices located on Γ1,∂ . This gauge transformation changes the value of the
holonomies along the edges of Γ1,∂ , and j can be choosed in such a way that these holonomies
take any prescribed values, provided the conjugacy classes of the holonomies along L1 , . . . , Lp
remain unchanged. So, we just need to prove that the value of I1 is preserved by the action of
such a gauge transformation. But this action also affects the holonomy along the other edges
that meet Γ1,∂ , so that the gauge invariance of f1 and D Γ1 is not sufficient to conclude. It is
the biinvariance of the Haar measure that allows at this point to forget about the effect of j on
the edges outside Γ1,∂ .

    The relation between conditional partition functions that was just established deserves to be
